SUBJECT: REDUCTION IN BUDGET EXPENDITURES FOR 194O-41.

GLOeT 25 1940
10/21/40
The Honorable
C
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that, according to press
reports, the Japanese Cabinet on September 3, 1940, ap-
proved a reduction in budget expenditures for the year
1940-41 amounting to ¥642,187,000. This represents slightly
more than 5 percent of the total budget of 11,338,000,000
(exclusive of special military appropriations) voted by
the Diet early in the year.
The

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The saving will include 319,064,000 in the general
account and 797,289,000 in the special account. However,
a duplication resulting from transfer of funds from one
account to another will reduce the net saving for both
accounts to the amount stated above, $642,187,000. More
than two-thirds of the reductions in the general account
and almost half of those in the special account are post-
poned rather than canceled expenditures.
The savings to be effected are announced as follows:
Canceled
Postponed

NET SAVING
642,187,000
It is doubtless true that some appropriations which
have been canceled or postponed were intended for bureaus
not yet created. Plans for increase of personnel in certain
offices have apparently been abandoned. In citing illustra-
tions of the savings to be effected, the Yomiuri mentions
the following: (1) the Foreign Office will abandon its
plan to appoint an additional official of "chokunin" rank
to the Embassy in China; (2) the Home Ministry will not ap-
point an additional official of "chokunin" rank to its
shrines

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
shrines bureau; (3) various engineering projeots have been
postponed; (4) the Ministry of Justice will not appoint
another "chokunin" procurator to handle thought offenses;
(5) the Agriculture and Forestry Ministry will re-examine
its plan for setting up a food bureau and will reduce rice
subsidies; (6) the Ministry of Commerce and Industry will
reduce subsidies; (7) the Ministry of Education will not
set up a physical training bureau; (8) the Ministry of
Communications will reduce appropriations for the Electricity
Board; and (9) the Overseas Ministry will reduce expendi-
tures for emigration.
Press reaction to the announcement of a budget saving
was generally favorable although there was some criticism
of the large proportion of "postponed" expenditures. Shares
on the Tokyo Stock Exchange fell on September 3, the key
bourse issue declining to the lowest price.since October,
l93l. The decline is attributed to a fear of deflation
i ndicated by the proposed reduced Government expenditures.
An editorial in the Yomiuri Shimbun criticizes the
Government for failing to effect more substantial cuts in
actual appropriations and for resorting to temporizing
measures in postponing expenditures until the next fiscal
year. "Were the Government only possessed of extraordinary
determination and zeal, it might have achieved a more
drastic budget reduction without resorting to such tempor-
izing measures as the postponement and carrying forward of
certain expenditures." The Yomiuri goes on to suggest that
the present reduotion should be an example to inspire greater
outs in next year's budget.
The

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
The Chugai Shogyo Shimpo points out editorially that
the present economy action is merely a superficial reduotion
and postponement of certain expenditures determined to be
unnecessary and does not represent the fundamental revision
of government finance so urgently needed at the present time.
The Hochi calls the sudden drop in the stock market
a temporary phenomenon whioh does not necessarily attest
to the soundness of Kawada's financial policy. While com-
monding the budget savings, it states that the role of the
Minister of Finance is greater than that of a budget balancer
and urges that, taking cognizance of the changes in the times
and in national finance, he assume positive economic leader-
ship in bringing about fundamental finanoial reforms.
The Nichi Nichi welcomes the curtailment, praising
especially the elimination of appropriations for certain
new bureaus. An undue increase in expenses for civil
service over the past two years is deplored.
With meagre information available as to the exact econo-
mies to be effected, it is difficult at this time to estimate
the effeot of the present budget reduotion on Japan's finan-
cial policy or position.
Respectfully youra,
Joseph C. Grew
X

JKE:nn

the maohinery in the form of'silk exported
to this country. I did not enter into a
dieoussion of this phase other than to say

which would in any way involve an extension
of credit. Mr. Thun said that he understood
our position.
I thanked Mr. Thun for the thoughtfulness
of his company in having referred to the De-
partment the matter under consideration and
said that we would make no written reply to
his letter and desired that my oral statement
be regarded as the Departmentis reply. I
also said that we hoped that the whole matter
could be handled without publioity and without
reference to the Department of State. I
suggested that, if there was any need to make

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
OCT 31 1940
DIVISION OF FAREASTERN AFFAIRS
October 30, 1940
PAH
aH.
It is suggested that either Mr. Hamilton
or Mr. Hornbeck telephone Mr. Ferdinand Thun
of the Textile Machine Works, Reading, Pennsyl-
vania, along lines as follows:
"The Department has received your letter
of October 21, 1940, in which you report that
Japanese silk interests have expressed the
desire of entering into negotiations with
your firm for the purchase of certain obsolete
knitting machines, the sale to involve an
extension of credit for a period of several
years.
"As a result of the operation of exchange
control in Japan and the difficulty of getting
funds out of Japan the Department of Commerce,
on June 6, 1938, advised exporters to have a
confirmed irrevocable letter of credit in
their hands before accepting orders for ship-
ments to Japan. The exchange situation in
Japan has, of course, become considerably
worse since that date. Moreover in the light
of the fact that Japan's military activities
in China have resulted and are continuing to
result in serious injury to American rights
and interests, it has been the hope of the
American Government that American businessmen,
on their own initiative and taking into
account

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
account the credit risk involved, would not
take any action which would facilitate, either
directly or indirectly, Japan's activities.
This Government regards the extension of credit
to Japanese interests as coming within that
category of action and accordingly this Govern-
ment has endeavored and continues to endeavor
to discourage the extension of credit to Japan
or for the use of Japanese interests in China.
In recent weeks the Japanese authorities in
central China have taken action which threatens
speedily to eliminate American participation in
the trade of that area; this action has been
particularly severe upon American participetion
in the silk trade and has involved a virtual
Japanese monopolization of the silk trade in
central China. That there is a connection
between recent actions of the Japanese to
monopolize the silk trade of central China
and the present efforts of Japanese silk in-
terests to purchase machinery on credit in this
country for use in central China is highly
probable.
894.51/689
FEC-JORSS:MHP

In a recent statement given out by the Finance
Minister it was announced that reduotions had been made
1n the expenditures for the ourrent fiscal year which will
result in a saving of ¥645,187,000. This amount is divided
between items in the present budget whioh have been can-
celled outright and those whose disbursement has been post-
poned.
2. The oaneelled and postponed items are indicated as
follows:

true saving therefor is as follows:

3.
Comment.
a. Although the above reductions in expenaitures are
referred to as "savings" by both the Finance inistry
and the press, they are only savings in the sense that
they represent reduotions in wnat will be spent in the
current fiscal year, since the expenditure of one por-
tion is only deferred and the sums whioh are canoelled
are to be kept by the government and transforred to
reserve funds. Thus, while they do not represent a
diminishing of the amount which must be wet by taxes or
other inoome in the course of the fiscal year, they do
represent savings in that they are reduotions in the
amounts whioh it was originally contemplated spending
on various items when the budget was originally drawn
up.
b. ith the usual vagueness which exists in statements
or this sort, it is impossible to determine just what of
the cancelled and postponed amounts will fall under
either the general or special accounts. ll that can
be definitely kuown is that during the remainder of the
present fiscal year the originally contemplated disburse-
ment of ¥10,557.341.434 (Report No. 10,088) wil1 be re-
duoed by ¥645,187,000 leaving a total of ¥9,912,154,434
as the budget for the fiscal year 1940-1941.
0. It appears that the cuts by whioh the above savings
have been made have been mainly in the Communications,
Railway, Korean, Formosan, Monopoly Fureau accounts as

well as from a group broadly classified as "Others"

d. The reductions accomplished as indicated above are
without doubt the result of some rather drastic pruning
by the present Cabinet which announced at the time of
its taking ofrice that it would commit itselr to the
greatest possible economy in government expenditures.
While it is true that this saving which amounts to
about 6 per cent of the total budget is not very large
when viewed against the background of the total appro-
priations for the fisoal year, it is considered as
something of an accomplishment in view of the faot that
the total budget for the year, whioh was drawn up by a
preceding cabinet, was given out as representing the
minimum possible under current conditions.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 30, 1940
:
I telephoned Mr. Arthur Becker of the
Department of Agriculture this morning to
ask whether he knew anything about the reported
sale of Australian flour to Mitsui. He
telephoned me back this afternoon and said
that the report was in substance correct but
that the sale had been made to the Mitsubishi
Company. His information came from a Mr. Olsen,
a representative of the Mitsubishi Company.
According to Mr. Olsen the terms of the trans-
action are one half cash in sterling at the
official sterling rate of exchange, and one
half to be paid at the end of four months,
4-1/2 percent interest to be paid on the unpaid
balance. Mr. Olsen said that Canada has in-
dicated her willingness to sell wheat on the
same terms.
Especially in view of the recent action
of this Department in cutting out its subsidy
payments on exports of wheat and flour to
Japanese interests and the recent approach of
this Government to the Australian Government
in regard to similar action by the Australian
Government, it is suggested that it would be
advisable to ask the American Minister in
Canberra to attempt to verify the report and
to indicate how the terms may differ, if at
all, from those of recent sales to Japanese
interests.

Spatch No. 34, dated October 3, 1940, frem
meriean Minister to Australia, on mubjest of
Australia of eredits to Japan, North Chine and
rehase of wheat end wheat flour.
dum of Conversation
September 25, 1940.
Subjeet: Extension by Australia of eredits
to Japan, North China and Dairen
for the purchase of wheat and
wheat flour.
Present: Mr. J. F. Murphy, Secretary, Department
of Commerce;
Mr. Gauss;
Mr. Minter.
On the occasion of our visit on September 24 to
the Department of Commerce, Mr. Gauss asked Mr. Murphy if
the Commonwealth Government intended to give any further
oredit to Japan for wheat and flour. The answer was in the
negative. The situation today is very unlike that of a
year ago when Australia had a large surplus. The orop this
year will be smaller and Australia is not pressed for overseas
markets. Moreover, the attitude of the present Minister of
Commeroe has changed (undoubtedly after considerable pressure
by his permanent officials). Mr. Murphy stated that
Minister Cameron had on recent occasion told a Japanese
commercial representative "You can't have war and wheat too."
He said that the Government agrees with the poliey of the
State Department in oonnection with such oredits to Japan,
for they also feel certain that cash would thereby be re-
leased for the purohase of materials for prosecuting & war
against China and porhaps other countries.
While alluding with some respeot to the polioy of the
United States, Mr. Murphy informed us that they had again
been advised that the Japanese were being given oredit by
Mew York concerns for shipments of wheat made under a
marantee from the Reconstruction Finance Corporation.
He explained that on a former occasion when such a report
reached them they had Macgregor (Australian trade commissioner
at New York) investigate and found that there was no truth
in the statement. The present report is being likewise
investigated in the same manner. Today, Mr. Murphy told
Mr. Minter that a report from Mr. Maogrogor in New York
contained a complete denial of any Reconstruotion Finance
Corporation or other official United States involvement in
wheat shipments to Japan.
While we were there Mr. Murphy gave us the following
statistios concerning the wheat and flour whieh were involved
in the earlier deals with Japan. Incidentally, both the
wheat and the flour were for use in North China. The first
deal 1nvolved 200,000 tons of wheat, half to be paid twelve
months after the first shipment left Australia and the re-
maining half six months later. The first shipment left
Australia in December 1939. The second transactiom imvolved
57,000 tons of flour. Terms of payment were one hals dowm,
eme quarter after twelve months, and one quarter eighteen
memths after the first shipment.

Hemorandum of Conversation
November 4, 1940.
Subjeet: Extension by Australia of oredits
to Japan, North China and Dairen

Present: Mr. J. F. Murphy, Seoretary, Department
of Commeroe;
Mr. Minter.
Upon receipt of the Department's telegram no. 12,
dated Nevember 1, 1940, 6 p.m., I attempted to got into
touch with Mr. Murphy, but was only able to reach him
Sunday afternoon by telephone. Mr. Murphy then gave
me briefly sufficient information to enable the Legation
to reply to the Department's telegram.
I talked with Mr. Murphy again today in order that
I might get a more complete history of the transastion,
and the following is substantially what Mr. Murphy told
For some weeks prior to the conversation which
Mr. Gaeuss and I had with Mr. Murphy on September 25, the
Wheat Board had been negotiating with the Mitsui Company
for the purohase of wheat. The Wheat Board had steadfastly
informed Mitoui that no further long-term credits would
be granted, Mitsui again having sought twelve to eighteen
months. During that period of negotiation the Wheat Board
had told Witsui that they would sell them wheat one half
cash, one half at the end or 120 days. Finally, about
September 1l, the Wheat Board gave Mitsui a l0-day option
to buy l00,000 tons on the forogoing terms, stating that
this would be the last credit of any kind that would be
granted. The option expired on the night of September 20,
a Friday. On September 21, the Mitsui representative
appeared at the Wheat Board and explained that had their
telegram of instruetions been promptly released by the
Australian sensors they would have taken up an option for
50,000 tons on September 19. When the Wheat Board asked
the Seeretary of the Department of Commeree for authority
to make the deal, the Seeretary instruoted the Board to
refuse. Mr. Murphy did not give us that infommation on
September 25, and now explains that he would have done so
had he expeeted to be overruled by the Minister for
Commeree.
Failing to secure satisfaetion from the permanent
officials, the Mitsui representative appromched the
Minister for Commeree and she latter put the matter
before the Cabinet at its next meeting about September 27.
The Minister or Supply revealed at this Cabinet meeting that
the Missui Company had recently aceommodated him by granting
swe

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
two or three days' extension on an option which had expired.
This option was for textiles which the Minister of Supply
required for distribution to the military. In the eiroum-
stanees the Cabinet declded that it would be the fair thing
te grant a similar extension to Mitmui. This was dome, but
it was made plein that no further such transaetions would
take plase.
When I told Mr. Murphy that our information from the
Department was to the effeet that it was the Mitmubishi
Cempany representative who had stated that the wheat in
question had been sold to his company, he seemed very much
sarprised. He stated that the Australian Government had
never negotiated with the Mitmubishi Company for the purehase
of wheat or flour, but only with the Mitsui Company.
Mr. Murphy said that it was his understanding that these
companies were bitter rivals, having no tie-up with eash
other, yet it has been his experienee that eaoh always
knows what the other is doing. Mr. Murphy thought that
the Mitsubishi representative in the United States might
have stated that he knew of this transaction but had not
made himselr olear as to the company with whioh the deal
had been made.
Mr. Murphy was able today to confirm that the interest
rate on the deal with the Mitsui Company was 4& percent
per annum.

two or three days' extension on an option which had expired.
This option was for textiles which the Minister of Supply
required for distribution to the military. In the ciroum-
stances the Cabinet decided that it would be the fair thing
to grant a similar extension to Mibsui. This was done, but
1t was made plain that no further such transaotions would
take place.
When I told Mr. Murphy that our information from the
Department was to the effect that it was the Mitsubishi
Company representative who had stated that the wheat in
question had been sold to his company, he seemed very much
surprised. He stated that the Austraiian Government ad
never negotiated with the Mitsubishi Gompany for the purohase
of wheat or flour, but only with the Miteui Company.
Mr. Murphy said that it was his understanding that these
companies were bitter rivals, having no tieup with each
other, yet it has been his experience that each always
knows what the other is doing. Mr. Murphy thought that
the Mitsubishi representative in the United States might
have stated that he knew of this transaction but had not
made himself clear as to the company with which the deal
had been made.
Mr. Murphy was able today to confirm that the interest
rate on the deal with the Mitsu1 Compeny was 4-1/2 percent
per annum.
J.R.M.
610/861.31
JRM/meg

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
January 13, 1941
MEMORANDUM
RE: TOKYO FIRE AND MARINE INSURANCE COMPANY
In early November the Tokyo Fire and Marine Insurance Company gave
orders to all its branch offices in the U. S. to close. They were instructed
to re-insure their business with American insurance firms. At this time this
company had about #200,000,000 of insurance in force in the U. S., along with
$12,000,000 in cash reserves. Branch offices were instructed to remit the
$12,000,000 reserves to the home office in Tokyo as soon as possible.
In the letter received by local representatives for the Tokyo Fire
and Marine Insurance Co., the company stated that their action was being
taken under compulsion of their government. Their cash reserves, locally,
amounted to $50,000 in the Yokohama Specie Bank and $20,000 in bonds with
local insurance commissioner.
The Insurance Department of the local representatives informed Tokyo
that their affairs would be closed as soon as possible and their balance
remitted.
On December 9, 1940, the following wire was received from the Tokyo
office:
"PLEASE EXPEDITE REMITTANCE OUR BALANCES"
On December 13, 1940, the following wire was received:
"WHEN WILL BONDS HE RELEASED BY INSURANCE
COMMISSIONER. WHEN CAN WE EXPECT REMITTANCE
BANK BALANCE AND RONDS. PLEASE TELEGRAPH REPLY"
On December 17, 1940, the following wire was received:
"PIEASE RENIT PANK BALANCE LARCEST POSSIRLE
AMCUNT, LEAVING RESERVE FOR PAYMENT TAX
ADJUSTIENT. E CONSIDER YOUR INSURANCE
COMMISSIONEPS REPORT TO NEW YORK INSUPANCE
SUPERINTENDENT NECESSARY IN ORDER TO HAVE
OUR STATUTORY DEPOSIT AND TRUSTEED FUNIS IN
NEW YORK LIPERATED FROM THEIR APPLICATION
TO HAWAII SO WE CAN OPTAIN COMPLFTE RELEASE
THESE FUNDS"
The above quoted wires indicate extreme haste on the part of the
Tokyo Company to get all their cash funds out of the United States.

ESTIMATED MONETARY GOLD STOCK OF JAPAN
January
It is of course possible that some of the recent
exports of gold from Japan may have consisted of gold
acquired by Germany from occupied countries. On the
other hand, there is no evidence from the studies of
the Japanese gold position that I have pursued continu-
394.5
ously for several years which would justify such a
conclusion.
The table on page 6 presents in summary form the
51/707
estimated monetary gold stock of Japan and the leading
factors of change affecting them from the beginning of
1937 to December 15, 1940. As shown in this table,
FILED
exports of gold from Japan have been relatively smalr
2
during this yeer, amounting to only $74,000,000 as c8in-
19
pared to $251,000,000 1n 1937, $201,000,000 1n 1938, nd
$171,000,000 in 1939. The distribution of gold exports
by months during 1940 is as follows:
PS/G C
(Millions

=== Page 188 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
(Millions of dollars)
January
February
10.7
March
4.7
April
4.7
May
3.4
June
July
August
3.9
September
21.1
October
12.2
November
4.2
December
8.7
(in trans1t)
Total
73.6
I believe there is a very strong presumption in favor
of the hypothesis that these exports of gold were covered
almost entirely by acquisitions of newly mined gold and .
possibly to some extent by small acquisitions of old gold
from private holders. There have been, of course, no official
statistics of Japanese gold acquisitions for over three years.
If newly mined gold purchased during 1940 was the same in amount
as I estimated for 1939, some $72,000,000 of newly mined
gold would have been acquired. While newly mined gold
might have been less than this, it is not improbable that
there were at least some acquisitions of old gold.
It may be noted that our Commercial Attache at Tokyo
in his special finencial report for the week ending Septem-
ber 21, 1940, pointed out that through September 24, 1940
total gold exports from Japan for that year amounted to
1,365,430 ounces, or approximately $48,000,000. The Com-
mercial Attache states that it is believed that these ship-
ments represent actual production, and that there has been
no

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
no substantial increase in production so far this year.
If these shipments actually represent gold produced during
the first three-quarters of 1940, an annual production
of approximately $60,000,000 would be indicated. On the
other hand, the Commercial Attache does not state that
there has been any decrease in gold production during
1940, and if the Japanese have continued to export only
newly acquired gold, a production about equivalent to
that of last year would be indicated. Pending the receipt
of any more definite information, which is unlikely, I
have estimated Japanese gold production during 1940 as
equivalent to that which I estimated for 1939, namely
$72,000,000, and have made no separate estimate for old
gold acquired as against estimated acquisitions of old
gold of some $12,000,000 which were estimated for 1939.
It may also be pointed out that in as much as I
estimated that monetary gold remaining in Japan amounted
to approximately $115,000,000 at the end of 1939, 1t would
seem clear that there would have been an ample amount of
gold to meet any possible deficit of new acquisitions of
gold relative to gold exports that may have occurred since
that time. This would be true even if my estimate of re-
maining Japanese gold reserves were in error by as much as
$100,000,000, and 1f only $60,000,000 of gold had been
produced during 1940.
viewing

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Viewing the four year period as a whole, it may be noted
from the table on page 6 that at the beginning of 1937
the monetary gold stock of Japan was reported as $463,000,000.
During this period, gold exports amounted to $697,000,000,
or $234,000,000 in excess of reserves at the beginning of
1937. During the same period, newly mined gold purchased
1s estimated at $257,000,000 and old gold purchased at
$91,000,000. In order to assume that Japan's own holdings
of gold have been completely exhausted so that recent ex-
ports of gold from Japan represent gold acquired from
Germany, it would be necessary to assume that my estimates
of Japanese gold production and acquisitions of old gold
have been excessive by more than one-third. In view of
the fact that these estimates during the last three years
have been little more than guesses based on what has been
reported to us from the field, I am willing to concede that
a fairly large margin of error is possible. However, I do
not believe that my estimates involve an error of such a
magnitude as 33-1/3 percent.
In this connection I should like to point out that
in a study of Japanese gold holdings prepared by Lieutenant
Towson for the War Department, he stated that the Treasury
Department estimated that in January of 1940, Japan had
less than $80,000,000 of gold remaining. This is of course
about $35,O00,000 less than my estimate. (The Treasury
Department's

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Department's estimate was made after the arrival of
some $38,000,000 of gold from Japan in January, 1940,
and hence 1ts estimate of $80,000,000 should be compared
to mine of $115,000,000 of gold remaining in Japan at
that time). Even if the smaller Treasury Department
estimate is accepted, there would have been enough gold
left in Japan at the end of January, 194o to cover gold
exports during the remainder of the year even if there
had been no acquisitions of newly mined gold during 1940
whatsoever.
GL
EA:LUTHRINGER:EHS

International Finance and Exehange
Compeny, Ineorporated.
Interview of
Consul F. W. Hinke
with
Dr. C. J. Fox, company's former
legal adviser in Tientsin.
This afternoon I called on Dr. Charles J. Fox,
former legal adviser to the International Finance
and Exehange Company, Incorporated, to inquire as
to the prosent status of this firm. Dr. Fox told
me that to his knowledge the firm is now inaotive
in Tientsin; that he had been approached by the
company in l935 to aot as its legal adviser and in
that eapaoity had induoed Mr. Herman Young and Mr.
Max Lorensen to act as "dummy" direotors; and that
he had applied in 1935 at this Consulate General for
registration of the company as an American firm al-
though he was aware at the time that the prinoipal
financial interests involved were Japanese and the
firm was not entitled to registration. Dr. Fox
mentioned he had arranged office accomodations for
the company at two different times, in l935 in the
Tientsin Trust Building and during 1938 in a build-
ing previously oooupied by the Italian Bank belong-
1ng to the Vordoni interests. He added that late
in 1939 he had been approaohed by Mr. Ciret, repre-
senting the Vordoni interests, because of the com-
pany's non-payment of rent. After writing repeated
letters to the company's head office in Tokyo (to
which no replies were received), Mr. Ciret finally
sold the oompany'e furniture still on the premises.
Dr. Fox reoalled that among the projeots con-
sidered by the company in 1935 was a premium lottery
schome, whieh he had advised the interested Japanese
was oontrary to Amerioan law. Ho added that they
had had other plans - some of them very ambitious,
but none of whioh materialized, despite the expendi-
ture of considerable sums of money here roughly
Yon 100,000.
From Dr. Fox I gathered the impression that
during the two periods when the company operated in
Tientsin, its local management was exolusively in
Japanese hands, and that none of the Amerioan figure-
heads wes aware fully or the company's projects or
plans and certainly did not participate in their
development or exeeution. He intimated that the
Japanese interests involved were quite proud of
having obtained Ameriean status through inoorporation
1n

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.95)
-2-
im Delaware, which they had maintained over a period
of many years. He believed that the firm still
maintained offices in Tokyo.
From the files in the possession of Dr. Fox which
I saw, there was nothing to indieate that the firm
was now doing business looally or that he, or the
other Americans formerly conneoted with it, had any
astive conneotion with it at the present time.

Government estimates under the General Account for the
fiscal year 1941-1942 amount to ¥6,863,000,000 an increase of

¥12,000,000,000.

JAPANESE GOVERNMENT ESTINATES - FISCAL YEAR 1941-42
The Cabinet, on December 10, 1940, approved the Govern-
ment estimates under the General Account for the fiscal year
194l-42. These estimates, as approved, will be submitted to
the Diet when it convenes about December 24, 1940.
The amounts allocated to the various Government depart-
ments, as announced in the press, are as follows:

A comparison of the budget figures for fiscal year l940-41
with fiscal year 194l-42 shows an increase of approximetely
¥1,000,000,000 in the total, of which the Navy's share is
¥213,000,000 (¥1,241,000,000 c0mpared t0 ¥1,028,000,000).
The
amount allocated to the Army and Navy is 38 percent of the
entire Regular Budget.
The above figures do not of course include the expenses
under "Extraordinary Military Expenses", which account is to be
submitted for the Government's approval early in January l94l.
For the fiscal year 1940-41, ¥4,460,000,000 was budgeted and
the press has announced that the sum for the coming fiscal year
will be even greater.
Further details concerning the Navy's Budget will probably
be announced leter and will be made the subject of another report.

I have the honor to enclose herewith a translation
894.51
of an article which appeared in "Solidaridad Nacional"
PS/LOC
(Barcelona daily and Falange party organ) of February
1, 194l, entitled "Finances, A Political Weapom".
This is an article from the Berlin correspondent
of "Solidaridad Nacional" transmitted through the Trans-
ocean Agency, and has reference to statements attributed
to Secretary Morgenthau. The Japanese Foreign Minister
is quoted as having stated in a recent speech that
loans have been repeatedly offered to the Japanese
MAR 1 5 1941
Government by the United States, but that as the object
FILED
of the proposed loans was neither humanitarian nor pure-
ly financial, but rather political, they were rejected.
It is added that the United States does not pursue this
"dollar diplomacy" exclusively with Japan, but has long
used it in South America. It is remarked that French
funds now blocked in the United States will eventually
be confiscated

be confiscated "if France's attitude does not agree
with Washington's desires", and that as British funds
in the United States are now exhausted the American
Treasury will "requisition British funds in South
America ... thus enabling the United States to recover
expenses and at the same time eliminate the English
brother from South America".

this Embassy's despatch No. 5l68 dated November 22,
1940, subject: "Japan's Gold Position". The only
additional statistics now available are those cover-
ing gold shipments during 1940, which show that
these aggregated 2,239,485.278 troy ounces or
69,648 kilos valued at Yen 328,953,425.48. Compara-
tive figures for 1939 are 4,819,739.970 ounces,
149,894 kilos, and Yen 660,958,733.81. These data
indicate a reduction of 53-1/2% in volume and 50% in
value.
Our estimates of collection for January-
October, 1940 may have been somewhat low as there are
rumors afloat to the effect that, although the
Government has not actually enforced the Gold Com-
pulsory Purchase Law promulgated in October, 1940,
a nationwide collection of gold articles is now
being meticulously carried out on the basis of the
census of gold holdings taken during 1938, and that
this pseudo collection is actually a confiscation of
holdings even though it is cloaked in patriotic garb.
However it is not believed that receipts of exportable
gold from this source during 1940 equalled the
estimate for 1939, but they may have reached some-
thing like 8,000 kilos.

during 1940 of 69,648 kilos and an estimated 8,000

EXPLANATORX STATEXENT BY MINXSTHX OF FXRANCE
For the purpose of conselidating the war-time
finanees and coonomies, along with plans to provide for
a ehamge in international relations and to consummate the
adjustment of the China inoident, it is deemed essential
to conduet state affairs so as to conform with the war-time
strmeture more than ever before. In the budget estimetes
of expenditures for the 194l-42 fiseal year, therefore,
amphasie is laid on a prinoiple of ourteilment and a prin-
eiple for acoelerating State undertakings of urgently im-
portant charaoter, and efforts will be made to carry out
nesessary measures for the establishment of a high dagree
defense State sueh as the replonishment of armaments and
the expansion of productive power mors vigorously as com-
pared with the preceding fisoal year. As regards other
measures, it is arranged that the launching of undertakings
of less importanee shall be postponed under the existing
situation exoept those which are urgently needed. Regarding
She already-fixed-contimuing expemditures, steps shall,
in erder to utilize the existing finansial strength as
effeetively es possible, be tekan to examine these expendi-
tures carefully and at the same time to re-organise the
systems of the Government offices and to either abolish or
adjust various osmmittees in shese offices. Expeelally
shall a rigid ourtailment be practissd over heavy expenditure
required for the eequisition of inportans materials and over
expenditures peyable abroad. In the budges estimates of

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.96)

revenues for the 1941-42 fisoal year, receipts mooruing from
taxation based on aotual results in recent years and other
properly-estimated amounts are itemized, besides an inereased
amount based on the taxation reform oarried out in the pre-
seding fisoal year. Concerning defioits in State finances,
there will be no alternative but to resort to the issue of
loans as heretofore in order to cover these deficits. On
the whole, the budget estimates of the 194l-42 fisoal year
are compiled on the basis of the follow items:-
(1) Necessary expenditures for the replenishment
of armanents.
(2) Expenditures urgently necossary for the re-
lief of military men, enoouragement of science,
expanelon of productive power, control of
economies, and development of trade, mritime
transportation and oivil aviation.
(3) Transfer to urgent-mllitary-expenditure-speciel
account of certain anounts out of a total of
ingroased azount acoruing from the taxation
reform sinee the year 1938 and of the inereased
amcunt in prcfits of the Nonopoly Bureau on

to a greater extent and a reduotlon of expen-

894.51/72
My dear Mr. Acheson:
netard
I appreciate your letter of June 20
replying to our inquiry on the extension of credit to
Baker and Company in connection with silver imported
from Japan.
We note that it is the policy of the
Department of State not to favor the extension of credits
to Japan. As our transactions involve no such credits,
894.51
but consist solely in financing for an American company
silver in transit after its arrival in the United States,
we feel that our activities are not in contravention of
any statutes, regulations or policies of the Government.
Thanking you for your attention to the
matter,
Sincerely yours,
PS/LR
Vice President.

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.95)
July 28, 1941
MBMORANDUM
Information of a confidential character has been
received to the effect that S. Uno, Japanese Assistant Manager
GN
of the Yokohame Specie Bank, had been in touch with the oper-
AnS
stor of an armored car service in San Francisco, with a proposi-
tion to him regarding the transfer of $5,000,000 worth of bonds
to the Tatuta Maru of the NIK Steamship Lines. It is contemplated
山
this transfer of bonds will be made by armored car on July 31,
1941, for shipment to Japan, Uno having stated the bonds were to
be plaoed in a special tank on the ship.
Uno cautioned the operator of the armored car service
whom he contacted, to keep this matter very confidential, and at
the time of this contact Uno appeared to be very much concerned.
It is understood, according to information emanating
from an employee of the Tokohama Specie Bank at San Francisco,
that some of their confidential records have been removed during
the past three months by means of the Japanese Transfer Company,
to boats sailing for Japan.
